On the near soliton dynamics for the 2D cubic Zakharov-Kuznetsov equations
Gong Chen, Yang Lan, Xu Yuan

Abstract
. In this article, we consider the Cauchy problem for the cubic (mass-critical) Zakharov-Kuznetsov equations in dimension two: ∂ t u + ∂ x 1 ( Δ ​ u + u 3 ) = 0 , ( t , x ) ∈ [ 0 , ∞ ) × ℝ 2 . \partial_{t}u+\partial_{x_{1}}(\Delta u+u^{3})=0,\quad(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}. For the initial data in H 1 H^{1} close to the soliton and satisfying a suitable space-decay property, we fully describe the asymptotic behavior of the corresponding solution. More precisely, for such initial data, we show that only three possible behaviors can occur: 1) The solution leaves a tube near soliton in finite time; 2) the solution blows up in finite time; and 3) the solution is global and locally converges to a soliton. In addition, we show that for initial data near a soliton with non-positive energy and above the threshold mass, the corresponding solution will blow up as described in Case 2. Our proof is inspired by the techniques developed for the mass-critical generalized Korteweg–de Vries (gKdV) equation in a similar context by Martel-Merle-Raphaël [ 35 ] . More precisely, our proof relies on refined modulation estimates and a modified energy-virial Lyapunov functional. The primary challenge in our problem is the lack of coercivity for the Schrödinger operator, which appears in the virial-type estimate. To overcome the difficulty, we apply a transform, which was first introduced in Kenig-Martel [ 13 ] , to perform the virial computations after converting the original problem into an adjoint one. The coercivity of the Schrödinger operator in the adjoint problem has been numerically verified by Farah-Holmer-Roudenko-Yang [ 9 ] .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main results

Consider the 2D cubic Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation,

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x_{1}}(\Delta u+u^{3})=0,\quad(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2},

(1.1)

where
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\Delta=\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}+\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}
is the Laplace operator on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Recall that, by the work of
[
5
,
16
,
25
,
53
]
, the Cauchy problem for equation (
1.1
) is locally well-posed in the energy space
H^{1}
: for any initial data
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, there exists a unique (in a certain sense) maximal solution of (
1.1
) in
C\left([0,T):H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\right)
with
u_{|t=0}=u_{0}
. Moreover, for this problem, the following blow-up criterion holds:

T<\infty\Longrightarrow\lim_{t\uparrow T}\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}=\infty.

(1.2)

For any
H^{1}
solution
u
, the mass
M
and energy
E
are conserved, where

M(u(t))=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|u(t,x)|^{2}{\rm d}x\quad\mbox{and}\quad E(u(t))=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|\nabla u(t,x)|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|u(t,x)|^{4}\right){\rm d}x.

Recall also that, for any solution
u
of (
1.1
) and
\lambda>0
, the scaling symmetry

u_{\lambda}(t,x)=\lambda u(\lambda^{3}t,\lambda x),\quad\mbox{for}\ (t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2},

again results in a solution to (
1.1
). This scaling symmetry keeps the
L^{2}
-norm invariant so that the problem is
mass-critical
.

Denote by
Q
the
ground state
, which is the unique radial positive solution of (
1.1
):

-Q^{\prime\prime}-\frac{Q^{\prime}}{r}+Q-Q^{3}=0,\quad Q^{\prime}(0)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{r\to\infty}Q(r)=0.

It is well-known and easily checked that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left|Q^{(n)}(r)\right|\lesssim r^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-r},\quad\mbox{for}\ r>1.

We refer to Berestycki-Lions
[
1
]
for the work related to the soliton
Q
. Using the symmetries of the equation, from
Q
, for any
(\lambda_{0},x_{1,0},x_{2,0})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, one can find the family of
soliton/traveling wave
solutions to (
1.1
):

u(t,x)=\lambda_{0}Q\left((\lambda_{0}(x_{1}-\lambda_{0}^{2}t-x_{1,0}),\lambda_{0}(x_{2}-x_{2,0})\right).

Based on a variational argument (see
[
1
,
21
,
56
]
),
the unique radial positive solution
Q
attains the best constant
C
in the following Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality

\|f\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\quad\mbox{for any}\ f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

It follows from the definition of the energy
E
that

E(u)\geq\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left(1-\frac{\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}{\|Q\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}\right),\quad\mbox{for any}\ u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Combining the above estimate with the conservation of the energy and blow-up criterion, we obtain the global existence for any initial data with
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}<\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
.

For the case of
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\geq\|Q\|_{L^{2}}
, the existence of blow-up solutions (in finite time or infinite time) has been an interesting problem and has attracted people’s attention in recent years. In particular, in this direction, the first result is obtained by Farah-Holmer-Roudenko-Yang
[
9
]
which focuses on the blow-up dynamics for the case that the mass is slightly above the threshold.
More precisely, they show that, there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
such that, for any initial data
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying

E(u_{0})<0\quad\mbox{and}\quad 0<\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}-\|Q\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\alpha_{0},

the corresponding solution
u(t)
blows up in finite or infinite forward time.

In this article, we study the soliton dynamics of the 2D Cauchy problem (
1.1
): we first prove the rigidity of the solution flow for the initial data near the soliton and then show a blow-up result for such solutions with non-positive energy. We start with the definition for the set of initial data and
L^{2}
-modulated tube near the soliton manifold.

Definition 1.1
.

For any
\alpha>0
, we define the
L^{2}
-modulated tube near the soliton manifold as follows:

\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}=\left\{u\in H^{1}:\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{0}>0\\
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{subarray}}\left\|u(\cdot)-\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}Q\left(\frac{\cdot-x_{0}}{\lambda_{0}}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}<\alpha\right\}.

Moreover, for any
\alpha>0
, we define the following initial data set:

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}=\left\{u_{0}=Q+\varepsilon_{0}:\|\varepsilon_{0}\|_{H^{1}}<\alpha\ \mbox{and}\ \int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\infty}y_{1}^{100}\varepsilon_{0}^{2}(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}y_{2}<1\right\}.

Our first main result is the following rigidity of the solution flow in
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

There exist some universal constants
0<\alpha\ll\alpha^{*}\ll 1
such that the following is true. Let the initial data
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
. Then for the corresponding solution
u(t)
of (
1.1
), one of the following three scenarios occurs
:

Exit:

There exists a finite time
T\in(0,\infty)
such that
u(T)\notin\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
.

Blow-up:

The solution
u(t)
blows up in finite time
T\in(0,\infty)
with

\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}=\frac{\ell(u_{0})+o_{t\uparrow T}(1)}{(T-t)^{\beta}},

where
\beta\in\left(\frac{5}{7},\frac{5}{6}\right)
is a universal constant and
\ell(u_{0})>0
is a constant depending only on
u_{0}
. Moreover, for all
t\in(0,T)
, we have
u(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
.

Soliton:

The solution
u(t)
is globally defined on
[0,\infty)
, and for all
t\in[0,\infty)
, we have
u(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha^{*}}
. Moreover, there exist some constants
(\lambda_{\infty},x_{2,\infty})\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
and a
C^{1}
function
x(t)=(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t))
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda_{\infty}u(t,\lambda_{\infty}\cdot+x(t))-Q\right\|_{H^{1}_{{\rm loc}}}\rightarrow 0,\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{ as }t\rightarrow\infty,

\displaystyle|\lambda_{\infty}-1|\lesssim\delta(\alpha)\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ x(t)\sim\left(\frac{t}{\lambda_{\infty}^{2}},x_{2,\infty}\right),\text{ as }t\rightarrow\infty.

Our second main result focuses on the blow-up dynamics near the soliton in
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

There exists a universal constant
0<\alpha\ll 1
such that the following is true. Let the initial data
u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
be such that

E(u_{0})\leq 0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|Q\|_{L^{2}}<\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}.

Then the corresponding solution
u(t)
blows up in finite time
T
in the regime described by Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 1.4
.

In the statement of Theorem
1.2
, the constant
\beta
is defined by

\beta=\frac{1}{3-\theta}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\theta=2\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\big|\widehat{F}(\xi)\big|^{2}}{1+|\xi|^{2}}{\rm d}\xi\bigg)\bigg/\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|\widehat{F}(\xi)\big|^{2}{\rm d}\xi\right).

(1.3)

Here, the functions
F
and
\widehat{F}
are given by

F(y_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widehat{F}(\xi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}F(y_{2})e^{-iy_{2}\xi}{\rm d}y_{2}.

Actually, the value of the constant
\theta
is quite essential in our analysis, since the blow-up rate is determined by it (see more discussion in §
1.3
). Using elementary numerical computations, we find
\theta\approx 1.66\in\left(\frac{8}{5},\frac{9}{5}\right)
which implies the blow-up rate
\beta\in\left(\frac{5}{7},\frac{5}{6}\right)
. We present the details of the numerical computation in Appendix
A
.

Remark 1.5
.

We point out that the
y_{1}^{100}
weight in Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
is merely a technical restriction, and we do not claim its sharpness. For a similar but more relaxed restriction in the context of the mass-critical gKdV equation, we refer to
[
35
, §1.3]
.

Remark 1.6
.

A detailed numerical study on blow-up for (
1.1
) and its the mass supercritical counterpart
\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x_{1}}(\Delta u+u^{4})=0
was presented in Klein-Roudenko-Stoilov
[
17
]
. In the mass-critical case, the authors of
[
17
]
conjectured that the blow-up happens in finite time and at spatial infinity. Moreover, the authors conjectured that the blow-up rate satisfies
\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\sim\left(T-t\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. In the mass-supercritical case, the authors of
[
17
]
conjectured that the blow-up happens in finite time and at a finite spatial location. Similar to the mass-critical problem, the authors also conjectured that the blow-up rate satisfies
\|\nabla u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\sim\left(T-t\right)^{-\frac{2}{9}}
. It is worth mentioning that our result is different from the conjecture that was checked by numerical blow-up computations. More precisely, our blow-up rate is faster than the one stated in their conjecture.

Remark 1.7
.

We mention here that very recently, a similar result was proved independently by the work Bozgan-Ghoul-Masmoudi
[
3
]
, using a similar but different method. Most importantly, the two works employ different energy-virial Lyapunov functionals. (see more discussion in §
1.3
and §
4
).

1.2.
Related results

In the last twenty years, there has been remarkable progress towards understanding the near soliton dynamics and singularity formation of nonlinear dispersive equations, particularly for the mass-critical gKdV and nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equations. For the gKdV equation, in the work of Martel-Merle-Raphaël
[
35
,
36
,
37
]
, the authors gave a complete description and classification of the solution flow near soliton that completes the previous results in
[
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
40
]
. Then, building on the work
[
35
]
, Martel-Merle-Nakanishi-Raphaël
[
34
]
constructed the threshold manifold near the soliton for gKdV equation. For the NLS equation, in the work of Bourgain-Wang
[
2
]
, the authors constructed a family of conformal blow-up solutions. Then, based on the series works
[
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
]
of Merle-Raphaël, a complete description of singularity formation for the solution flow near soliton was obtained. We refer to
[
14
,
15
,
19
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
39
,
47
,
48
]
for some related results of mass-critical models. We also refer to
[
11
,
12
,
20
,
46
,
51
,
52
,
54
]
and references therein for some related results of energy-critical models, which are natural analogies of mass-critical models.

We now briefly survey the literature related to the Zakharov-Kuznetsov models. The Zakharov-Kuznetsov equations are natural extensions of the gKdV equations in higher dimensions and are of physical importance. For more historical and physical background, we refer to the introductions by Farah-Holmer-Roudenko-Yang
[
8
,
9
]
. The local and global well-posedness theory of the Zakharov-Kuznetsov models with various nonlinear powers and in different dimensions has been studied extensively. Without attempting to be exhaustive, we refer to the works
[
5
,
16
,
25
,
53
]
and the references therein for details.

For the 2D mass-critical problem (
1.1
), the instability of the soliton was obtained by Farah-Holmer-Roudenko
[
6
]
. This matches the situation with the critical gKdV equation. Again in the mass-critical case, when the initial data has negative energy with the mass slightly above the threshold, Farah-Holmer-Roudenko-Yang
[
9
]
showed that the gradient of the solution blows up in finite or infinite forward time. Finally, we would like to mention that for the 2D mass super-critical problems, the instability of soliton was shown by Farah-Holmer-Roudenko
[
7
]
.

For the 2D quadratic Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation, after passing to the adjoint problem without regularization, the asymptotic stability of soliton and stability of multi-solitons have been proven by Côte-Muñoz-Pilod-Simpson
[
4
]
using virial and monotonicity estimates. Their virial estimates relied on a sign condition verified numerically. However, these numerical computations do not hold for the problem in the 3D case. Recently, Farah-Holmer-Roudenko-Yang derived in
[
8
]
a new virial estimate in the case of 3D, based on different orthogonality conditions, converting to an adjoint problem with regularizations and relying on the numerical analysis of the spectra of a linear operator. This allowed them to extend the asymptotic stability result of soliton to the 3D quadratic Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation. In the work of Mendez-Muñoz-Poblete-Pozo
[
38
]
, some new virial estimates in the cases of 2D and 3D are used to prove the decay of solutions in large time-dependent spatial regions. We mention here that, the existence and uniqueness of asymptotic multi-solitons were shown by Valet
[
55
]
in the cases of 2D and 3D using the strategy introduced by Martel
[
26
]
for the gKdV equation.
Very recently, the asymptotic stability of multi-solitons to the 2D and 3D quadratic Zakharov-Kuznetsov equations was established in Pilod-Valet
[
49
]
, and then, the same authors described the collision of two nearly equal solitary waves on the whole time interval and proved the stability of this phenomenon in
[
50
]
.

1.3.
Comments on the proof

The method of the current article, based on the use of the energy-virial Lyapunov functional, is inspired by the remarkable work
[
35
]
in a similar context for the mass-critical gKdV equation. The first step of the method is the decomposition of solution
u
for (
1.1
) close to a soliton in
H^{1}
:

u(t,x)=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)}\left(Q_{b(t)}+\varepsilon\right)\left(t,\frac{x-x(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right).

Here,
Q_{b}
is close to
Q
for
b
small enough. In the current case (see also
[
35
, §2.2]
for the case of gKdV equation), we can write the function
Q_{b}
as

Q_{b}=Q+bP\phi_{b},

where
\phi_{b}
is a suitable localized profile and
P
is a non-localized profile to be determined.
As usual in investigating the blow-up phenomenon of mass-critical problem, we introduce the following new variables:

s=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}(\tau)}{\rm d}\tau\quad\mbox{and}\quad y=\frac{x-x(t)}{\lambda(t)}.

Now, the sharp description of the near soliton dynamics relies on the determination of the finite-dimensional dynamical system for a suitable choice of geometrical parameters
(\lambda(t),b(t),x(t))
, coupled to the infinite-dimensional dynamics related to the reminder term
\varepsilon(t)
. Roughly speaking, in our analysis, we handle the finite-dimensional dynamics via standard ODE argument and then handle the infinite-dimensional dynamics via energy-virial Lyapunov functional.

However, the presence of an additional dimension and the non-explicit soliton expression in our problem introduce several challenges compared to the case of gKdV equation.
A direct problem we encounter when studying the 2D Cauchy problem, using the general strategy introduced by
[
35
]
, is that we have to solve a different elliptic equation when constructing the non-localized profile
P
since an extra dimension
x_{2}
exists. This directly leads to the difference in the finite-dimensional system for the geometrical parameters (
\lambda,b
). Roughly speaking, in our case now, the geometrical parameters
(\lambda,b)
satisfy

\frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}t}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}},\quad\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}=-b\quad\mbox{and}\quad b_{s}+\theta b^{2}=0.

(1.4)

Equation (
1.4
) differs slightly from the case of gKdV equation (see
[
35
, Page 67]
). It is worth mentioning that due to the presence of the constant
\theta
, we obtain a slightly different dynamic for the solutions, which results in a different blow-up rate. This may reflect the influences and interactions arising from the additional dimension. More precisely, from (
1.4
), we directly have

\frac{\lambda_{t}}{\lambda}=-\frac{b}{\lambda^{3}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad b_{t}=-\theta\frac{b^{2}}{\lambda^{3}}\Longrightarrow\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}t}\left(\frac{b}{\lambda^{\theta}}\right)=0.

Formally, from a standard ODE argument, we obtain the following three scenarios for the dynamics of the solution with initial data
(\lambda,b)_{|t=0}=\left(1,b_{0}\right)
:

(i)

For the case of
b_{0}<0
, we have
\lambda(t)=\left(1-(3-\theta)b_{0}t\right)^{\frac{1}{3-\theta}}
on
[0,\infty)
and the dynamic is stable.

(ii)

For the case of
b_{0}=0
, we have
\lambda(t)\equiv 1
on
[0,\infty)
and the dynamic is unstable.

(iii)

For the case of
b_{0}>0
, we have
\lambda(t)=\left(b_{0}(3-\theta)\left(T-t\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{3-\theta}}
on
[0,\infty)
where
T=(b_{0}(3-\theta))^{-1}>0
and the dynamic is stable.

Another, and more significant, problem we encounter when studying the 2D Cauchy problem is the lack of coercivity for the Schrödinger operator in the energy-virial estimate for the original remainder term
\varepsilon
. Heuristically, when we compute the time variation of the virial quantity
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y_{1}\varepsilon^{2}{\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}y_{2}
, the following Schrödinger operator
\mathcal{B}
will appear in the estimate:

\mathcal{B}=-\frac{3}{2}\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{2}Q^{2}+3y_{1}Q\partial_{y_{1}}Q.

In contrast to the case of the gKdV equation (see
[
29
, Proposition 4]
and
[
35
, Lemma 3.4]
), the coercivity of the operator
\mathcal{B}
under suitable orthogonality conditions has not yet been obtained. In particular, the analysis and numerical verification for the 2D operator seems to be significantly more challenging than in the 1D case. To overcome this difficulty, inspired by the work of
[
9
]
, we employ a transform introduced in Kenig-Martel
[
13
]
to reduce the original problem to an adjoint one related to a new term
\eta
. To the best of our knowledge, this technique was first introduced in the context of the gKdV equation by Martel
[
27
]
.
Here, we consider the regularized dual problem
\eta=(1-\gamma\Delta)^{-1}\mathcal{L}\varepsilon
where
\gamma
is a sufficiently small constant and
\mathcal{L}=-\Delta+1-3Q^{2}
is the linearized operator around the soliton
Q
. Actually, when we compute the time variation of the virial quantity
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y_{1}\eta^{2}{\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}y_{2}
, the following Schrödinger operator
\mathcal{A}
will appear in the estimate:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}f

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}f-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}f+\frac{1}{2}f-\left(\frac{3}{2}Q^{2}+3y_{1}Q\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)f

\displaystyle+3\frac{(f,y_{1}Q)}{(Q,Q)}Q^{2}\partial_{y_{1}}Q+3\frac{(f,Q^{2}\partial_{y_{1}}Q)}{(Q,Q)}y_{1}Q,\quad\mbox{for any}\ f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

We mention here that, under suitable orthogonality conditions, the coercivity of the operator
\mathcal{A}
has been verified in
[
9
, §16]
through numerical computation. Therefore, even though we do not know whether the suitable coercivity of the operator
\mathcal{B}
exists, we can establish a virial estimate for the regularized dual problem of
\eta
based on the coercivity of the operator
\mathcal{A}
. Then, by combining the energy estimate of
\varepsilon
with the virial estimate of
\eta
, we obtain the energy-virial Lyapunov functional with monotonicity, and thus, we could obtain the control of the infinite-dimensional term over the whole space. We point out that the weight functions appearing in the energy and virial quantities must be chosen carefully, as the constant dependence on the time variation of such quantities differs and should be handled attentively. (see more details in §
4.1
–§
4.3
). Last, we also point out that the regularized transformation we apply here also reminisces the Darboux transformations which were successfully applied to study the asymptotic stability of kinks and solitons in various problems on any compact interval (see for instance
[
18
,
28
]
).

1.4.
Outline of the article

The article is organized as follows. First, Section
2
introduces the technical tools involved in the choice of the localized profile
Q_{b}
: spectral theory of the linearized operator, the pointwise estimates of the non-localized profile
P
and the localized profile
Q_{b}
. Then, Section
3
introduces the technical tools involved in a dynamical approach to the soliton problem for (
1.1
): geometric decomposition near soliton and the modulation estimates for the geometric parameters. Next, Section
4
focuses on the establishment of the energy-virial Lyapunov functional that plays a crucial role in our analysis.
Finally, by the monotonicity of the energy-virial Lyapunov functional and a suitable bootstrap argument, Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
are proved in Section
5
and Section
6
, respectively.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation and Conventions

For any
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we set

|\alpha|=|\alpha_{1}|+|\alpha_{2}|\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{y}^{\alpha}=\frac{\partial^{|\alpha|}}{\partial_{y_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}}.

We denote by
\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the set of smooth function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
r_{n}>0
and
C_{n}>0
such that

\sum_{|\alpha|=n}|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}f(y)|\leq C_{n}(1+|y|)^{r_{n}}e^{-|y|},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{d}.

We also denote by
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the set of smooth function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist
r_{n}>0
and
C_{n}>0
such that

\sum_{|\alpha|=n}|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}f(y)|\leq C_{n}(1+|y|)^{r_{n}}e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{d}.

For any
(f,g)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we introduce the following
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
function

f\otimes g:y=(y_{1},y_{2})\longmapsto f(y_{1})g(y_{2}).

The Fourier transform of a function
h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, denoted by
\mathcal{F}h
or
\widehat{h}
, is defined as:

\mathcal{F}h(\xi)=\widehat{h}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(y)e^{-iy\cdot\xi}{\rm d}y,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{d}.

The Fourier transform defines a linear isometric operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, that is,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|h(y)|^{2}{\rm d}y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\widehat{h}(\xi)|^{2}{\rm d}\xi,\quad\mbox{for any}\ h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The inverse Fourier transform of a function
h
, denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{-1}h
, is defined as:

\mathcal{F}^{-1}h(y)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(\xi)e^{iy\cdot\xi}{\rm d}\xi,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{d}.

Recall that, we denote by
Q(y):=Q(|y|)
the unique radial positive solution of (
1.1
):

-Q^{\prime\prime}-\frac{Q^{\prime}}{r}+Q-Q^{3}=0,\quad Q^{\prime}(0)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\lim_{r\to\infty}Q(r)=0.

Based on the ODE arguments, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C_{n}>0
such that

\sum_{|\alpha|=n}\left|\partial^{\alpha}_{y}Q(y)\right|\lesssim e^{-|y|},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

Recall also that, from a variational argument,
the unique radial positive solution
Q
attains the best constant
C
in the following Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality

\|f\|_{L^{4}}^{4}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla f\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\quad\mbox{for any}\ f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

We denote the linearized operator around
Q
by

\mathcal{L}f=-\Delta f+f-3Q^{2}f,\quad\mbox{for}\ f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

Next, we introduce the scaling operator:

\Lambda f=f+y\cdot\nabla f,\quad\mbox{for}\ f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

In this article, we set

F(y_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{1},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(2.1)

For a given small constant
\alpha
, we denote by
\delta(\alpha)
a generic small constant with

\delta(\alpha)\to 0,\quad\mbox{as}\ \alpha\to 0.

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we denote the
L^{2}
-inner product by

(f,g)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(y)g(y){\rm d}y.

Then, for any
T\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we denote by
\left<T,f\right>
the canonical duality pairing between the distribution
T
and the test function
f
.

In our analysis, we always need to carefully trace the dependence on a large-scale constant
B
. We set the following conventions: the implied constants in
\lesssim
and
O
are
independent of

B
from Section
2
to Section
4
and can
depend on
the large constant
B
in Section
5
and Section
6
.

2.2.
The linearized operator

In this subsection, we recall the spectral theory of the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}
and then introduce the useful function for the construction of the localized profile.

Proposition 2.1

(Spectral properties of
\mathcal{L}
)
.

{\rm{(i)}}

Spectrum
. The self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{L}
has essential spectrum
[1,\infty)
, a unique single negative eigenvalue
-\mu_{0}
with
\mu_{0}>0
, and its kernel is
{\rm{Span}}\left(\partial_{y_{1}}Q,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right)
. Let
Y
be the
L^{2}
normalized eigenvector of
\mathcal{L}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
-\mu_{0}
. It holds, for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,

\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}Y(y)\right|\lesssim e^{-|y|},\quad\mbox{on}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

{\rm{(ii)}}

L^{2}
-scaling identities
. We have

\mathcal{L}\Lambda Q=-2Q\quad\mbox{and}\quad(Q,\Lambda Q)=0.

{\rm{(iii)}}

First coercivity.
There exists
\mu>0
such that, for all
f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

(\mathcal{L}f,f)\geq\mu\|f\|^{2}_{H^{1}}-\frac{1}{\mu}\left((f,Y)^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{1}}Q)^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{2}}Q)^{2}\right).

{\rm{(iv)}}

Second coercivity.
There exists
\nu>0
such that, for all
f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

(\mathcal{L}f,f)\geq\nu\|f\|^{2}_{H^{1}}-\frac{1}{\nu}\left((f,Q^{3})^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{1}}Q)^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{2}}Q)^{2}\right).

{\rm(v)}

Inversion of
\mathcal{L}
.

Let
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be such that
\left|(g,\nabla Q)\right|=0
. Then there exists a unique
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\mathcal{L}f=g
and
\left|(f,\nabla Q)\right|=0
. Moreover, if
g
is even in either
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, then
f
is also even in
y_{1}
or
y_{2}
. In addition, if
g\in\mathcal{Y}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
, then we have
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Proof.

Proof of (i)–(ii). The properties of
\mathcal{L}
in (i)-(ii) are standard and easily checked. We refer to
[
6
, Theorem 3.1 and Lemma 3.2]
for the details of the proofs.

Proof of (iii)–(iv). First, from
[
6
, Lemma 3.6]
, there exists
\tau>0
such that, for all
f\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

\left(\mathcal{L}f,f\right)\geq\tau\left\|f\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\frac{1}{\tau}\left((f,Y)^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{1}}Q)^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{2}}Q)^{2}\right).

Therefore, for any
0<\delta\ll 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}f,f\right)

\displaystyle=\delta\left(\mathcal{L}f,f\right)+(1-\delta)\left(\mathcal{L}f,f\right)

\displaystyle\geq\delta\|f\|_{{H}^{1}}^{2}+\tau(1-\delta)\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-3\delta\left(Q^{2},f^{2}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\tau}\left((f,Y)^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{1}}Q)^{2}+(f,\partial_{y_{2}}Q)^{2}\right),

which completes the proof of (iii) by taking
0<\delta\ll 1
small enough. The proof of (iv) is directly based on a similar argument to the one above and
[
6
, Lemma 3.5]
.

Proof of (v). We define

\displaystyle L^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\left\{v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\left|(v,\nabla Q)\right|=\left(v,Y\right)=0\right\},

\displaystyle H^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\left\{v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\left|(v,\nabla Q)\right|=\left(v,Y\right)=0\right\}.

First, for any
g\in L^{\perp}
, the map
M_{1}
which is defined by

\displaystyle M_{1}:h\longmapsto(h,g),\quad\mbox{for}\ h\in H^{\perp},

is a linear bounded functional. On the other hand, from (iii) of Proposition
2.1
, we know that the map
M_{2}
which is defined by

M_{2}:(f_{1},f_{2})\longmapsto\left(\mathcal{L}f_{1},f_{2}\right),\quad\mbox{for}\ (f_{1},f_{2})\in H^{\perp}\times H^{\perp},

is an inner product. Therefore, from Riesz representation Theorem, for any
g\in L^{\perp}
, there exists unique
f\in H^{\perp}
such that

\left(\mathcal{L}f,h\right)=\left(g,h\right),\quad\mbox{for any}\ h\in H^{\perp}.

It follows from
(g,f)\in L^{\perp}\times H^{\perp}
that

\left(\mathcal{L}f,h\right)=\left(g,h\right),\ \ \mbox{for any}\ h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\Longrightarrow\mathcal{L}f=g,\ \ \mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

(2.2)

Second, for any
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
\left|(g,\nabla Q)\right|=0
, we decompose

g=g^{\perp}+aY,\ \ \mbox{where}\ g^{\perp}\in L^{\perp}\ \mbox{and}\ a=(g,Y).

Using (
2.2
), we find, there exists unique
f^{\perp}\in H^{\perp}
such that
\mathcal{L}f^{\perp}=g^{\perp}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It follows that

f=f^{\perp}-\frac{a}{\mu_{0}}Y\ \mbox{with}\ |\left(f,\nabla Q\right)|=0\Longrightarrow\mathcal{L}f=g,\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

Then, the uniqueness of
f
is a direct consequence of
{\rm{Ker}}\mathcal{L}={\rm{Span}}\left\{\partial_{y_{1}}Q,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right\}
. Note that, from the uniqueness of
f
, we obtain, if
g
is even in either
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, then
f
is also even in
y_{1}
or
y_{2}
.

Third, taking Fourier transforms on the both sides of
\mathcal{L}f=g
, we have

\widehat{f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\left(3\widehat{Q^{2}f}(\xi)+\widehat{g}(\xi)\right),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2},

which implies

f(y)=\left[\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\right)\ast(3Q^{2}f+g)\right](y),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

(2.3)

On the one hand, from standard elliptic arguments, we have

f\in\bigcap_{k=1}^{\infty}H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\Longrightarrow 3Q^{2}{f}+g\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

(2.4)

On the other hand, for any regular function
h\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we find

\displaystyle\left<\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\right),\widehat{h}\right>

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{h(\xi)}{1+|\xi|^{2}}{\rm d}\xi=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)e^{-\rho|y|^{2}}{\rm d}y\right){\rm d}\rho.

By an elementary computation,

\mathcal{F}\left(e^{-\rho|y|^{2}}\right)(\xi)=(2\rho)^{-1}e^{-\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{4\rho}},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

It follows from the Plancherel Theorem that

\left<\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\right),\widehat{h}\right>=\frac{1}{2}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{-1}e^{-\left(\rho+\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{4\rho}\right)}{\rm d}\rho\right)\widehat{h}(\xi){\rm d}\xi\right].

Based on the above identity, we know that

\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\right)(y)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho^{-1}e^{-\left(\rho+\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\rho}\right)}{\rm d}\rho\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\left\{0\right\}\right).

Moreover, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
|y|>1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\right)(y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{\frac{|y|}{8}}\rho^{-1}e^{-\left(\rho+\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\rho}\right)}{\rm d}\rho

\displaystyle+\int^{2|y|}_{\frac{|y|}{8}}\rho^{-1}e^{-\left(\rho+\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\rho}\right)}{\rm d}\rho+\int_{2|y|}^{\infty}\rho^{-1}e^{-\left(\rho+\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\rho}\right)}{\rm d}\rho\lesssim e^{-|y|}.

Combining the above estimate with (
2.3
)–(
2.4
), we conclude that
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
∎

Recall that, we set

F(y_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{1},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

By an elementary computation, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{{\rm d}^{n}F}{{\rm d}y_{2}^{n}}(y_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{|y_{1}|\leq|y_{2}|}\left(1+|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|\right)e^{-\sqrt{y_{1}^{2}+y^{2}_{2}}}{\rm d}y_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{|y_{1}|>|y_{2}|}\left(1+|y_{1}|+|y_{2}|\right)e^{-\sqrt{y_{1}^{2}+y^{2}_{2}}}{\rm d}y_{1}\lesssim(1+|y_{2}|^{2})e^{-|y_{2}|},

which means that
F\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})
.

For future reference, we denote by
h_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})
the even solution of the following second-order ODE:

-h_{2}^{\prime\prime}(y_{2})+h_{2}(y_{2})=F^{\prime\prime}(y_{2}),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(2.5)

Then, we set

G:y_{1}\longmapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{2}(y_{2})Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{2}.

We fix a regular function
h_{1}\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{1}(y_{1}){\rm d}y_{1}=1
and
h_{1}
is orthogonal to
G
in the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
sense. It follows that

\left(h_{1}\otimes h_{2},Q\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{1}(y_{1})\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}h_{2}(y_{2})Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{2}\right){\rm d}y_{1}=0.

(2.6)

We now introduce the following non-localized profile for future reference.

Lemma 2.2

(Non-localized profile)
.

There exists a smooth function
P\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
\partial_{y_{1}}P\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}P=\Lambda Q,\quad\lim_{y_{1}\to\infty}\partial_{y_{2}}^{n}P(y_{1},y_{2})=0,\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N},

(2.7)

|(\nabla P,Q)|=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad(P,Q)=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|F(y_{2})|^{2}{\rm d}y_{2}.

(2.8)

Moreover, for any
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, there exists
C_{1\alpha}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}P(y_{1},y_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1\alpha}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}P(y_{1},y_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1\alpha}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}},\quad\ \mbox{on}\ (0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}.

For any
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
with
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
, there exists
C_{2\alpha}>0
such that

\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}P(y_{1},y_{2})\right|\leq C_{2\alpha}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}},\quad\ \mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}^{2}.

(2.10)

Proof.

We consider
P\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of the form

P(y_{1},y_{2})=\widetilde{P}(y_{1},y_{2})-\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho-h_{2}(y_{2})\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}h_{1}(\rho){\rm d}\rho.

with
\widetilde{P}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
By the definition of
h_{2}
, we see that
\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}P=\Lambda Q
is equivalent to

\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}\widetilde{P}=\Lambda Q+\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}\int_{y_{1}}^{+\infty}\left(\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2})+h_{1}(\rho)h_{2}(y_{2})\right){\rm d}\rho=\partial_{y_{1}}R,

(2.11)

where

\displaystyle R(y)=

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{1}}\Lambda Q-\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho-3Q^{2}\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}_{1}(y_{1})h_{2}(y_{2})+F^{\prime\prime}(y_{2})\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}h_{1}(\rho){\rm d}\rho-3Q^{2}\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}h_{1}(\rho)h_{2}(y_{2}){\rm d}\rho.

First, on
(y_{1},y_{2})\in(1,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, from
\left(h_{1},h_{2},F\right)\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})
and the definition of
\Lambda Q
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
r_{n}>0
, such that

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=n}\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}R(y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int^{\infty}_{y_{1}}\left(1+\left(\rho^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{r_{n}}{2}}\right)e^{-\sqrt{\rho^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}}{\rm d}\rho

\displaystyle+|y|^{r_{n}}e^{-|y|}\lesssim(1+|y|)^{r_{n}+1}e^{-|y|}.

Second, on
(y_{1},y_{2})\in(-\infty,-1)\times\mathbb{R}
, from the definition of
h_{1}
and
F
, we see that

\lim_{y_{1}\to-\infty}\left(F^{\prime\prime}(y_{2})\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}h_{1}(\rho){\rm d}\rho-\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)=0.

Based on the above identity and the Fundamental theorem, on
(y_{1},y_{2})\in(-\infty,-1)\times\mathbb{R}
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
r_{n}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=n}\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}R(y)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\left(1+\left(\rho^{2}+y_{2}^{2}\right)^{\frac{r_{n}}{2}}\right)e^{-\sqrt{\rho^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}}{\rm d}\rho

\displaystyle+|y|^{r_{n}}e^{-|y|}\lesssim(1+|y|)^{r_{n}+1}e^{-|y|}.

Combining the above two estimates, we obtain
R\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

On the other hand, since
R(y_{1},y_{2})
and
Q(y_{1},y_{2})
are both even in
y_{2}
, we have
(R,\partial_{y_{2}}Q)=0
. Then, using (
2.11
),
\partial_{y_{1}}Q\in{\rm{Ker}}\mathcal{L}
and (ii) of Proposition (
2.1
),

\left(R,\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)=-(\Lambda Q,Q)+\left(\int_{y_{1}}^{+\infty}\left(\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2})+h_{1}(\rho)h_{2}(y_{2})\right){\rm d}\rho,\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)=0.

Therefore, from (v) of Proposition
2.1
, there exists
\widetilde{P}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\mathcal{L}\widetilde{P}=R\ \ \mbox{with}\ \left|\left(\widetilde{P},\nabla Q\right)\right|=0\Longrightarrow\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}\widetilde{P}=\partial_{y_{1}}R\ \ \mbox{with}\ \left|\left(\widetilde{P},\nabla Q\right)\right|=0.

Moreover, from
h_{2}(y_{2})
and
R(y_{1},y_{2})
are even in
y_{2}
and (v) of Proposition
2.1
, we see that
P
is also even in
y_{2}
.

Note that, from the definition of
P
and
\widetilde{P}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{y_{1}\to\infty}\partial_{y_{2}}^{n}P(y_{1},y_{2})

\displaystyle=\lim_{y_{1}\to\infty}\partial_{y_{2}}^{n}\widetilde{P}(y_{1},y_{2})-\lim_{y_{1}\to\infty}h^{(n)}_{2}(y_{2})\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}h_{1}(\rho){\rm d}\rho

\displaystyle-\lim_{y_{1}\to\infty}\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}\partial_{y_{2}}^{n}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho=0.

Note also that, using (
2.6
) and the definition
P
, we see that

\left(\partial_{y_{1}}P,Q\right)=-\left(\widetilde{P},\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)+\left(\Lambda Q,Q\right)+\left(h_{1}\otimes h_{2},Q\right)=0.

In addition, from
P(y_{1},y_{2})
and
Q(y_{1},y_{2})
are even in
y_{2}
, we have
(\partial_{y_{2}}P,Q)=0
.
Next, from (ii) of Proposition
2.1
,
\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}P=\Lambda Q
and
\mathcal{L}P\to 0
as
y_{1}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\left(P,Q\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathcal{L}P,\Lambda Q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{y_{1}}^{\infty}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho,\Lambda Q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2})\Lambda Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\textbf{1}}_{\{y_{1}\leq\rho\}}{\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}\rho\right){\rm d}y_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{1}\right)^{2}{\rm d}y_{2}=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|F(y_{2})|^{2}{\rm d}y_{2}.

Last, the estimate (
2.9
) with
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
, the second line of estimate (
2.9
) and the estimate (
2.10
) are direct consequences of
\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}P\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. On the other hand, for the case of
\alpha_{1}=0
, from
(h_{1},h_{2},\Lambda Q,\widetilde{P})\in\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, there exists
r_{\alpha_{2}}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}P(y_{1},y_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+|y_{1}|^{r_{\alpha_{2}}}+|y_{2}|^{r_{\alpha_{2}}})e^{-\frac{\sqrt{y_{1}^{2}+y^{2}_{2}}}{2}}+(1+|y_{2}|^{r_{\alpha_{2}}})e^{-|y_{2}|}

\displaystyle+\int^{\infty}_{y_{1}}\left(1+|\rho|^{r_{\alpha_{2}}}+|y_{2}|^{r_{\alpha_{2}}}\right)e^{-\sqrt{\rho^{2}+y^{2}_{2}}}{\rm d}\rho\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}}.

The proof of Lemma
2.2
is complete.
∎

2.3.
The localized profile

In this subsection, we introduce a localized profile to avoid the growth of
P
as
y_{1}\to-\infty
.
Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\phi\in[0,1]
with
\phi^{\prime}\geq 0
on
\mathbb{R}
and

\phi(y_{1})=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0,\quad\mbox{for}\ y_{1}<-2,\\
&1,\quad\mbox{for}\ y_{1}>-1.\end{aligned}\right.

For any
|b|\ll 1
, we now define the localized profile

\phi_{b}(y_{1})=\phi(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1})\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q_{b}(y)=Q(y)+bP(y)\phi_{b}(y_{1}).

Then the following estimates related to the localized profile hold.

Lemma 2.3
.

There exists a small constant
0<b^{*}\ll 1
such that for any
|b|<b^{*}
, the following estimates hold.

(i)

Estimate of
Q_{b}
. For all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle|Q_{b}(y)|

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}+|b|e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,0]}(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1}),

(2.12)

\displaystyle|\partial_{y_{1}}^{k}Q_{b}(y)|

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}+|b|^{1+\frac{3k}{4}}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,-1]}(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1}).

(ii)

Estimate for the error term
. Let

\Psi_{b}=-b\Lambda Q_{b}+\partial_{y_{1}}\left(-\Delta Q_{b}+Q_{b}-Q_{b}^{3}\right).

(2.13)

Then, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Psi_{b}(y)\right|\lesssim

\displaystyle|b|^{2}\left(e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}+e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,0]}(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1})\right)

(2.14)

\displaystyle+|b|^{\frac{7}{4}}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,-1]}(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1}),

\displaystyle|\partial_{y_{1}}^{k}\Psi_{b}(y)|\lesssim

\displaystyle|b|^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}+|b|^{1+\frac{3}{4}(k+1)}e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,-1]}(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1}).

(iii)

Scalar product with
Q
. We have

(\Psi_{b},Q)=-\frac{b^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widehat{F}(\xi)|^{2}}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}\xi+O\left(|b|^{3}\right).

(2.15)

(iv)

Energy and mass of
Q_{b}
. We have

\left|E(Q_{b})+b(P,Q)\right|\lesssim b^{2}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q_{b}^{2}{\rm d}y-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q^{2}{\rm d}y-2b(P,Q)\right|\lesssim|b|^{\frac{5}{4}}.

(2.16)

Proof.

Proof of (i). The estimate (
2.12
) follows directly from Lemma
2.2
.

Proof of (ii). By (
2.7
) and an elementary computation, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{b}=

\displaystyle-b(1-\phi_{b})\Lambda Q+b\left(\left(\mathcal{L}P-2\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}P\right)\phi^{\prime}_{b}-3(\partial_{y_{1}}P)\phi^{\prime\prime}_{b}-P\phi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{b}\right)

\displaystyle-b^{2}\left(3\partial_{y_{1}}\left(QP^{2}\phi^{2}_{b}\right)+(\Lambda P)\phi_{b}+y_{1}P\phi^{\prime}_{b}\right)-b^{3}\partial_{y_{1}}\left(P^{2}\phi_{b}^{3}\right).

Then, the estimate (
2.14
) also follows directly from Lemma
2.2
.

Proof of (iii). From the definition of
\Psi_{b}
and decay properties of
P
and
Q
,

(\Psi_{b},Q)=-b^{2}\left(\partial_{y_{1}}\left(3QP^{2}\right)+\Lambda P,Q\right)+O\left(|b|^{3}\right).

Using (
2.7
) and integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle(\Lambda P,Q)=-(P,\Lambda Q)

\displaystyle=\left(P,\Delta\partial_{y_{1}}P-\partial_{y_{1}}P+\partial_{y_{1}}(3Q^{2}P)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(P,\Delta\partial_{y_{1}}P-\partial_{y_{1}}P\right)-\left(\partial_{y_{1}}\left(3QP^{2}\right),Q\right).

From (
2.5
) and the definition of
P
in Lemma
2.2
, we know that

\displaystyle\left(P,\partial_{y_{1}}^{3}P\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{y_{1}}\left((\partial_{y_{1}}P)^{2}\right){\rm d}y=0,

\displaystyle\left(P,\partial_{y_{1}}P\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(P^{2}(y_{1},y_{2})\bigg|^{y_{1}=+\infty}_{y_{1}=-\infty}\right)\,{\rm d}y_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(F(y_{2})+h_{2}(y_{2})\right)^{2}{\rm d}y_{2},

\displaystyle(P,\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}\partial_{y_{1}}P)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\left(\partial_{y_{2}}P\right)^{2}(y_{1},y_{2})\bigg|^{y_{1}=+\infty}_{y_{1}=-\infty}\right){\rm d}y_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(F^{\prime}(y_{2})+h^{\prime}_{2}(y_{2})\right)^{2}{\rm d}y_{2}.

Taking the Fourier transform on the both sides of (
2.5
), we deduce that

\left(1+|\xi|^{2}\right)\widehat{h_{2}}(\xi)=-|\xi|^{2}\widehat{F}(\xi),\ \ \mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}\Longrightarrow\widehat{F}(\xi)+\widehat{h_{2}}(\xi)=\frac{\widehat{F}(\xi)}{1+|\xi|^{2}},\ \ \mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Combining the above identities with the Plancherel theorem,

(P,\Delta\partial_{y_{1}}P-\partial_{y_{1}}P)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+|\xi|^{2})\left(\widehat{F}(\xi)+\widehat{h_{2}}(\xi)\right)^{2}{\rm d}\xi=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|\widehat{F}(\xi)|^{2}}{1+|\xi|^{2}}\,{\rm d}\xi.

We see that (
2.15
) follows from the above identities.

Proof of (iv). By an elementary computation and integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle E(Q_{b})

\displaystyle=E(Q)+b\left(P\phi_{b},-\Delta Q-Q^{3}\right)+O(|b|^{2}),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q_{b}^{2}{\rm d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q^{2}{\rm d}y+b^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}P^{2}\phi_{b}^{2}{\rm d}y+2b\left(P\phi_{b},Q\right).

Combining the above identities with (
2.9
),
E(Q)=0
,
-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{3}=0
and the decay properties of
Q
, we complete the proof of (
2.16
).
∎

3.
Modulation estimates

3.1.
Geometric decomposition and Bootstrap assumptions

In this subsection, we recall a standard decomposition result on solutions of (
1.1
) that are close to the soliton manifold. More precisely, we assume that there exist
(\overline{\lambda}(t),\overline{x}(t),\overline{\varepsilon}(t))\in(0,+\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that, for all
t\in[0,t_{0})
, the solution
u(t)
of (
1.1
) satisfies

u(t,x)=\frac{1}{\overline{\lambda}(t)}\left[{Q}+\bar{\varepsilon}(t)\right]\left(\frac{x-\bar{x}(t)}{\bar{\lambda}(t)}\right),\quad\mbox{with}\quad\|\bar{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\kappa\leq\kappa^{*},

(3.1)

where
0<\kappa^{*}\ll 1
is small enough universal constant.

We now recall the following modulation result for solutions of (
1.1
).

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
u(t)
be a solution of (
1.1
) satisfying (
3.1
) on
[0,t_{0})
.
Then there exist
C^{1}
functions
(\lambda(t),x(t),b(t))\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that, for all
t\in[0,t_{0})
,
\varepsilon(t)
being defined by

\varepsilon(t,y)=\lambda(t)u(t,\lambda(t)y+x(t))-Q_{b(t)}(y),

(3.2)

it satisfies the orthogonality conditions

(\varepsilon(t),Q)=(\varepsilon(t),Q^{3})=|(\varepsilon(t),\nabla Q)|=0.

(3.3)

Moreover, we have

\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{L^{2}}+|b(t)|+\bigg|1-\frac{\overline{\lambda}(t)}{\lambda(t)}\bigg|\lesssim\delta(\kappa)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|\varepsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\delta(\|{\varepsilon}(0)\|_{H^{1}}).

Proof.

The proof of the decomposition proposition relies on a standard argument based on Proposition
2.1
, Lemma
2.2
and the implicit function Theorem.
For the sake of completeness, we provide a sketch here. Define the functional

(u,\Gamma)\longmapsto\Theta(u,\Gamma):=\left((\varepsilon,Q),(\varepsilon,Q^{3}),(\varepsilon,\partial_{x_{1}}Q),(\varepsilon,\partial_{x_{2}}Q)\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{4},

where
\Gamma=\left(\lambda,x,b\right)
.
We compute the Jacobian matrix of the above mapping with respect to
(\lambda,x,b)
and evaluate it at
(u,\lambda,x,b)=(Q,\overline{\lambda},\overline{x},0)
. Up to a rescaling and translations,
the heart of the proof is the invertibility of the Jacobian matrix:

\mathcal{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\left(\Lambda Q,Q^{3}\right)&\left(\Lambda Q,Q\right)&\left(\Lambda Q,\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)&\left(\Lambda Q,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right)\\
\left(P,Q^{3}\right)&\left(P,Q\right)&\left(P,\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)&\left(P,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right)\\
\left(\partial_{y_{1}}Q,Q^{3}\right)&\left(\partial_{y_{1}}Q,Q\right)&\left(\partial_{y_{1}}Q,\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)&\left(\partial_{y_{1}}Q,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right)\\
\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,Q^{3}\right)&\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,Q\right)&\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)&\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right)\end{array}\right).

Actually, by an elementary computation, we obtain

\mathcal{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{1}{2}\|Q\|_{L^{4}}^{4}&0&0&0\\
\left(P,Q^{3}\right)&\frac{1}{4}\|F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}&0&\left(P,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right)\\
0&0&\|\partial_{y_{1}}Q\|^{2}_{L^{2}}&0\\
0&0&0&\|\partial_{y_{2}}Q\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\end{array}\right),

which implies
\mathcal{M}
is invertible. See more details in the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 4.4]
and also see the proof of
[
35
, Lemma 2.5]
.
∎

As usual in investigating the blow-up phenomenon of mass-critical dispersive equations, we introduce the following new time variable

s=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}(\sigma)}{\rm d}\sigma\Longleftrightarrow\frac{{\rm d}s}{{\rm d}t}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}(t)}.

Recall that, we define

\Psi_{b}=-b\Lambda Q_{b}+\partial_{y_{1}}\left(-\Delta Q_{b}+Q_{b}-Q_{b}^{3}\right).

In addition, we set

\displaystyle{\rm{Mod}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)\left(\Lambda Q_{b}+\Lambda\varepsilon\right)-b_{s}\frac{\partial Q_{b}}{\partial b}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{x_{1s}}{\lambda}-1\right)(\partial_{y_{1}}Q_{b}+\partial_{y_{1}}\varepsilon)+\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}(\partial_{y_{2}}Q_{b}+\partial_{y_{2}}\varepsilon).

We now deduce the equation of
\varepsilon
from (
1.1
) and (
3.2
).

Lemma 3.2

(Equation of
\varepsilon
)
.

The function
\varepsilon
satisfies

\partial_{s}\varepsilon=\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}\varepsilon-b\Lambda\varepsilon+{\rm{Mod}}+\Psi_{b}-\partial_{y_{1}}R_{b}-\partial_{y_{1}}R_{\rm NL},

where

R_{b}=3(Q_{b}^{2}-Q^{2})\varepsilon\quad\mbox{and}\quad R_{{\rm{NL}}}=(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}-3Q_{b}^{2}\varepsilon-Q_{b}^{3}.

Proof.

We denote

v(t,y)=\lambda(t)u(t,\lambda(t)y+x(t)).

Using (
1.1
), we see that

\lambda^{3}\partial_{t}v+\partial_{y_{1}}\left(\Delta v+v^{3}\right)-\lambda^{2}\lambda_{t}\Lambda v-\lambda^{2}x_{t}\cdot\nabla v=0.

Based on the above identity and the definition of the time variable
s
, we have

\partial_{s}v+\partial_{y_{1}}\left(\Delta v+v^{3}\right)-\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}\Lambda v-\frac{x_{s}}{\lambda}\cdot\nabla v=0.

Therefore, from
v(s,y)=\varepsilon(s,y)+Q_{b(s)}(y)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\partial_{y_{1}}\left(-\Delta(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)+(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)-(Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{3}\right)+{\rm{Mod}}-b(\Lambda Q_{b}+\Lambda\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}\varepsilon-b\Lambda\varepsilon+{\rm{Mod}}+\Psi_{b}-\partial_{y_{1}}R_{b}-\partial_{y_{1}}R_{\rm NL}.

∎

For
i=0,1,2
, we define the smooth function
\varphi_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
as follows,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{i}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2},&\mbox{for}\ y_{1}\in(-\infty,\frac{1}{2}),\\
y_{1}^{i+6},&\mbox{for}\ y_{1}\in(1,+\infty),\end{cases}\quad\vartheta^{\prime}(y_{1})\geq 0,\ \ \mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Moreover, we define the smooth even function
\zeta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\zeta\in(0,1]
as follows,

\displaystyle\zeta(y_{1})=\begin{cases}e^{2y_{1}},&\mbox{for}\ y_{1}\in(-\infty,-\frac{1}{6}),\\
1,&\mbox{for}\ y_{1}\in(-\frac{1}{10},\frac{1}{10}),\\
e^{-2y_{1}},&\mbox{for}\ y_{1}\in(\frac{1}{6},\infty),\\
\end{cases}\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta(y_{1}){\rm d}y_{1}=1.

Let
B>100
be a large enough universal constant to be chosen later. For
i=0,1,2
, we define the following weight function,

\vartheta_{i,B}(y_{1})=\vartheta_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

We also define a smooth function
\psi_{B}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\lim_{y_{1}\rightarrow-\infty}\psi_{B}(y_{1})=0\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \psi_{B}^{\prime}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{B}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}<-\frac{1}{3}B,\\
\frac{1}{B}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\frac{1}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}\geq-\frac{1}{3}B.\end{cases}

Last, for
i=0,1,2
, we set

\varphi_{i,B}(y_{1})=\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}\vartheta_{i,B}(y_{1}),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Lemma 3.3
.

For all large enough
B>100
, the following estimates hold.

(i)

We know that
\psi_{B}
is strictly increasing and
\psi_{B}(y_{1})\to\frac{1}{2}
as
y_{1}\to\infty
.

(ii)

For all
y_{1}\in(-\infty,-B)
, we have

e^{\frac{2y_{1}}{B}}\leq\psi_{B}(y_{1})\leq 2e^{\frac{2y_{1}}{B}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}e^{\frac{y_{1}}{B}}\leq\varphi_{i,B}(y_{1})\leq e^{\frac{y_{1}}{B}}.

(iii)

For all
y_{1}\in\left(-\frac{B}{4},\frac{B}{4}\right)
, we have

\psi_{B}^{\prime}(y_{1})+\left|\psi_{B}(y_{1})-\frac{1}{2}\right|+\sum_{i=1,2}\left(\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}(y_{1})+\left|\varphi_{i,B}(y_{1})-\frac{1}{2}\right|\right)\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{6}B^{\frac{1}{3}}}.

(iv)

For all
y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
i=0,1,2
, we have
\psi_{B}(y_{1})\leq\varphi_{i,B}(y_{1})
.

Proof.

Proof of (i). First, from
\psi^{\prime}_{B}>0
on
\mathbb{R}
, we know that
\psi_{B}
is strictly increasing.
Then, from
\zeta
is an even function and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\zeta(y_{1}){\rm d}y_{1}=1
, we obtain

\int_{-\infty}^{0}\zeta(y_{1}){\rm d}y_{1}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\zeta(y_{1}){\rm d}y_{1}=\frac{1}{2},

which implies

\displaystyle\lim_{y_{1}\rightarrow+\infty}\psi_{B}(y_{1})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi^{\prime}_{B}(y_{1})\,{\rm d}y_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{B}{3}}\frac{1}{B}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right){\rm d}y_{1}+\int_{-\frac{B}{3}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{B}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\frac{1}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right){\rm d}y_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}}\zeta(y_{1}){\rm d}y_{1}+B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\zeta(y_{1}){\rm d}y_{1}=\frac{1}{2}-\int_{-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}}^{0}\zeta(y_{1})\,{\rm d}y_{1}+\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}=\frac{1}{2}.

Proof of (ii). For all
y_{1}<-B
, we have
\frac{y_{1}}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\leq-\frac{1}{6}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\psi_{B}(y_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\zeta\left(\frac{\rho}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right){\rm d}\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\frac{1}{B}\exp\left(\frac{2\rho}{B}+\frac{2}{3}-B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right){\rm d}\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(\frac{2}{3}-B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right)e^{\frac{2y_{2}}{B}}\in\left[e^{\frac{2y_{1}}{B}},2e^{\frac{2y_{1}}{B}}\right].

Based on the above estimate and the definition of
\varphi_{i,B}
, for
y_{1}<-B
, we find

\varphi_{i,B}(y_{1})=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}\in\left[\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}e^{\frac{y_{1}}{B}},e^{\frac{y_{1}}{B}}\right].

Proof of (iii). For all
y_{1}\in\left(-\frac{B}{4},\frac{B}{4}\right)
, we have

\frac{y_{1}}{B^{2}}\in\left(-\frac{1}{4B},\frac{1}{4B}\right)\subset\left(-\infty,\frac{1}{2}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\frac{1}{3}B^{\frac{2}{3}}\in\left(\frac{1}{12}B^{\frac{1}{3}},\frac{7}{12}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\subset\left(\frac{1}{6},\infty\right).

It follows from the definition of
\psi_{B}
and
\varphi_{i,B}
that

\psi^{\prime}_{B}(y_{1})+\sum_{i=1,2}\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}(y_{1})\lesssim\frac{1}{B}\exp\left(-\frac{2y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}-\frac{2}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right)\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{6}B^{\frac{1}{3}}},

and

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{B}(y_{1})-\frac{1}{2}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{-\frac{B}{4}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{B}\exp\left(-\frac{2\rho}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}-\frac{2}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right){\rm d}\rho\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{6}B^{\frac{1}{3}}},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,2}\left|\varphi_{i,B}(y_{1})-\frac{1}{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}-1\right|=\left|\frac{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})-1}{\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}+1}\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{1}{6}B^{\frac{1}{3}}}.

Proof of (iv). From
0<\psi_{B}<\frac{1}{2}
on
\mathbb{R}
and the definition of
\psi_{B}
and
\varphi_{i,B}
, we complete the proof of (iv).
∎

Based on the above lemma, we obtain the following technical lemma related to the pointwise estimates
\psi_{B}
and
\varphi_{i,B}
and their derivatives.

Lemma 3.4
.

The following estimates hold.

(i)

First-type estimates of derivatives of
\psi_{B}
. We have

B^{\frac{2}{3}}|\psi^{\prime\prime}_{B}|+B^{\frac{4}{3}}|\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{B}|\lesssim\psi^{\prime}_{B},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(ii)

Second-type estimates of derivatives of
\psi_{B}
.
For
i=1,2
, we have

\sqrt{B\psi^{\prime}_{B}}\lesssim B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(iii)

Third-type estimates of derivatives of
\psi_{B}
.
For
i=1,2
, we have

|y_{1}|\psi^{\prime}_{B}\lesssim\sqrt{\psi_{B}}\lesssim B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(iv)

First-type estimates of derivatives of
\varphi_{i,B}
. For
i=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle|\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{i,B}|

\displaystyle\lesssim B^{-\frac{2}{3}}\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+B^{-20}\psi_{B},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R},

\displaystyle|\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i,B}|

\displaystyle\lesssim B^{-\frac{4}{3}}\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+B^{-30}\psi_{B},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(v)

Second-type estimates on derivatives of
\varphi_{i,B}
.
For
i=1,2
, we have

B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B}\lesssim\varphi_{i-1,B}\lesssim B^{10}\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

(vi)

Third-type estimates on derivatives of
\varphi_{i,B}
.
For
i=1,2
, we have

\varphi_{i,B}\lesssim B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B}+|y_{1}|\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}\mathbf{1}_{[B^{10},\infty)},\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

Proof.

Proof of (i). From the definition of
\psi_{B}
, we see that

\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{B^{2}}\zeta^{\prime}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}<-\frac{1}{3}B,\\
\frac{1}{B^{\frac{5}{3}}}\zeta^{\prime}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\frac{1}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}\geq-\frac{1}{3}B,\end{cases}

\psi_{B}^{\prime\prime\prime}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{B^{3}}\zeta^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}<-\frac{1}{3}B,\\
\frac{1}{B^{\frac{7}{3}}}\zeta^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\frac{1}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}\geq-\frac{1}{3}B.\end{cases}

We see that the estimate in (i) directly follows from the above identities.

Proof of (ii) and (iii). Using again the definition of
\psi_{B}
and
\varphi_{i,B}
, we have

B\psi_{B}^{\prime}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}<-\frac{1}{3}B,\\
\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\frac{1}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}\geq-\frac{1}{3}B,\end{cases}

|y_{1}|\psi_{B}^{\prime}(y_{1})=\begin{cases}\frac{|y_{1}|}{B}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2}B^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}<-\frac{1}{3}B,\\
\frac{|y_{1}|}{B}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{\frac{2}{3}}}+\frac{1}{3}B^{\frac{1}{3}}\right),&\text{ for }y_{1}\geq-\frac{1}{3}B,\end{cases}

and

\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}(y_{1})=\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{B}(y_{1})}{\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}}\vartheta_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)+\frac{1}{B^{10}}\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}\vartheta^{\prime}_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right).

Therefore, from (i) of Lemma
3.3
, we complete the proof of (ii) and (iii).

Proof of (iv). Using again the definition of
\varphi_{i,B}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{i,B}(y_{1})}{\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{B}}{2\psi_{B}}-\left(\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{B}}{2\psi_{B}}\right)^{2}\right)\vartheta_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{B^{10}}\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{B}}{2\psi_{B}}\vartheta_{i}^{\prime}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)+\frac{1}{B^{20}}\vartheta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right),\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i,B}(y_{1})}{\sqrt{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}_{B}(y_{1})}{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}-3\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{B}(y_{1})\psi^{\prime\prime}_{B}(y_{1})}{(2\psi_{B}(y_{1}))^{2}}+3\left(\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{B}(y_{1})}{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}\right)^{3}\right)\vartheta_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{B^{10}}\left(\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}_{B}(y_{1})}{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}-\left(\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{B}(y_{1})}{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}\right)^{2}\right)\vartheta^{\prime}_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{B^{20}}\frac{\psi^{\prime}_{B}(y_{1})}{2\psi_{B}(y_{1})}\vartheta^{\prime\prime}_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)+\frac{1}{B^{30}}\vartheta^{\prime\prime\prime}_{i}\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right).

Therefore, using again the definition of
\psi_{B}
and
\varphi_{i,B}
, we complete the proof of (iv).

Proof of (v)–(vi). Combining the above identities with (i) and (ii) of Lemma
3.3
, we complete the proof of (v)–(vi).
∎

Recall that, from the definition of
Q_{b}
, we have

\frac{\partial Q_{b}}{\partial{b}}(y)=P(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial{b}}\left(b\phi\left(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1}\right)\right)=P(y)\left(\phi+\frac{3}{4}y_{1}\phi^{\prime}\right)\left(|b|^{\frac{3}{4}}y_{1}\right).

It follows from Lemma
2.2
that

\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{2}\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}\frac{\partial Q_{b}}{\partial b}\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{3}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,0]}(|b|^{\frac{4}{3}}y_{1})+e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y_{1}).

Based on the above estimate, Lemma
2.2
, Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
, we obtain the following pointwise estimates.

Lemma 3.5
.

The following estimates hold.

(i)

First-type weighted estimates for
Q_{b}
.
It holds

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{2}\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}Q_{b}\right|\left(\psi_{B}^{\prime}+\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+|\psi_{B}-\varphi_{i,B}|\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}}\left(B^{-30}+|b|\right)\left(B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B}\right).

(ii)

Second-type weighted estimates for
Q_{b}
.
It holds

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{2}\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}\frac{\partial{Q}_{b}}{\partial{b}}\right|\left(\psi_{B}+\psi^{\prime}_{B}+\varphi_{i,B}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,0]}(|b|^{\frac{4}{3}}y_{1})+e^{-\frac{|y|}{4}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y_{1})\right)\left(B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B}\right).

(iii)

Third-type weighted estimates for
Q_{b}
.
For any
\Gamma\in\left\{\Lambda,\nabla\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{2}\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}(\Gamma Q_{b}-\Gamma Q)\right|\left(\psi_{B}+\psi^{\prime}_{B}+\varphi_{i,B}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|\left(e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}}\mathbf{1}_{[-2,0]}(|b|^{\frac{4}{3}}y_{1})+e^{-\frac{|y|}{4}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y_{1})\right)\left(B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B}\right).

(iv)

Weighted estimates for
\Psi_{b}
.
It holds

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|=0}^{2}\left|\partial_{y}^{\alpha}\Psi_{b}\right|\left(\psi_{B}^{\prime}+\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+|\psi_{B}-\varphi_{i,B}|\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{4}}\left(B^{-30}+|b|\right)\left(B\varphi^{\prime}_{i,B}+\psi_{B}\right).

Proof.

The proof of the above estimates relies on an argument based on Lemma
2.3
, Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
, and we omit it.
∎

For
i=0,1,2
, we now define the following weighted
H^{1}
norms of
\varepsilon
,

\mathcal{N}_{i}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|\nabla\varepsilon(s,y)|^{2}\psi_{B}(y_{1})+|\varepsilon(s,y)|^{2}\varphi_{i,B}(y_{1})\right){\rm d}y.

Let
u(t)
be a solution of (
1.1
) satisfying (
3.1
) on
[0,t_{0}]
, and hence the geometrical decomposition in Proposition
3.1
holds on
[0,t_{0}]
. Let
0<\kappa\ll 1
be a small enough universal constant. We denote by
s_{0}=s(t_{0})
and assume the following priori bounds hold for all
s\in[0,s_{0}]
:

(i)

Scaling invariant bounds. We assume

|b(s)|+\mathcal{N}_{2}(s)+\|\varepsilon(s)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\kappa.

(3.4)

(ii)

Bounds related to
H^{\frac{\theta}{2}}
scaling. We assume

\frac{|b(s)|+\mathcal{N}_{2}(s)}{\lambda^{\theta}(s)}\leq\kappa.

(3.5)

Here, the value of
\theta
is given by (
1.3
).

(iii)

Decay assumption on the
y_{1}
-variable. We assume

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{0}^{\infty}y_{1}^{100}\varepsilon^{2}(s,y){\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}y_{2}\leq 10\bigg(1+\frac{1}{\lambda^{100}(s)}\bigg).

(3.6)

We mention here that, the bootstrap assumption (
3.4
)–(
3.6
) plays a crucial role in our proof. See more details related to the bootstrap assumption in Section
4
–
5
.

3.2.
Modulation estimates

In this subsection, we deduce the modulation estimates for the geometric parameters from the equation of
\varepsilon
and the orthogonal conditions. Recall that, in this article, we still assume that
u(t)
is a solution of (
1.1
) which satisfies (
3.1
) on
[0,t_{0}]
and thus admits on
[0,t_{0}]
a decomposition (
3.2
) as in Proposition
3.1
. Recall also that, we always denote
s_{0}=s(t_{0})
.

We start with the following standard energy and modulation estimates.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume that for all
s\in[0,s_{0}]
, the solution
u(t)
with initial data
u_{0}
satisfies the bootstrap assumption (
3.4
)–(
3.6
).
Then the following estimates hold.

(i)

Estimate induced by the conservation law.
We have

\displaystyle\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(u^{2}_{0}-Q^{2}\right){\rm d}y\right|,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\left|2\lambda^{2}E(u_{0})-\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y+(\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+|b|^{\frac{1}{4}})\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(ii)

Standard modulation estimates
. We have

\displaystyle|b_{s}|

\displaystyle\lesssim b^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+\left|\frac{x_{1s}}{\lambda}-1\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}\right|\lesssim b^{2}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(iii)

Weighted
L^{1}
estimate
. For all
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\left|\varepsilon(y_{1},y_{2})\right|\left|\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right|\right){\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}y_{2}\lesssim\left(B^{10}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Proof of (i). First, from the mass conservation law, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}^{2}{\rm d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q_{b}^{2}{\rm d}y-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q^{2}{\rm d}y-2b(P,Q)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}{\rm d}y+2b(P,Q)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q^{2}{\rm d}y+2b(\varepsilon,\phi_{b}P).

Combining the above identity with (
2.16
),
\|\phi_{b}P\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim|b|^{-\frac{3}{4}}
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain the first estimate in (i).

Second, from the energy conservation law, we find

\displaystyle 2\lambda^{2}E(u_{0})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\varepsilon|^{2}{\rm d}y+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon\left(-\Delta(Q_{b}-Q)-(Q_{b}^{3}-Q^{3})\right){\rm d}y-2b(P,Q)

\displaystyle+\left(2E(Q_{b})+2b(P,Q)\right)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left((Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4Q_{b}^{3}\varepsilon\right){\rm d}y.

Note that

\displaystyle-\Delta(Q_{b}-Q)=-b\phi_{b}\Delta P-2b\phi^{\prime}_{b}\partial_{y_{1}}P-b\phi^{\prime\prime}_{b}P

\displaystyle-(Q_{b}^{3}-Q^{3})=-3b\phi_{b}PQ^{2}-3b^{2}\phi_{b}^{2}P^{2}Q-b^{3}\phi_{b}^{3}P^{3}.

Hence, from (
3.4
) and the decay property of
P
in Lemma
2.2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon\left(-\Delta(Q_{b}-Q)\right){\rm d}y\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\|\nabla(\phi_{b}P)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim|b|^{\frac{5}{8}}\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim|b|+|b|^{\frac{1}{4}}\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

and

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon\left(-(Q_{b}^{3}-Q^{3})\right){\rm d}y\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+|b|^{3}\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}\|\phi_{b}^{3}P^{3}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim|b|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y.

Next, from the Gagliardo-Nirenberg’s inequality and the definition of
Q_{b}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left((Q_{b}+\varepsilon)^{4}-Q_{b}^{4}-4\varepsilon Q_{b}^{3}\right){\rm d}y\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim|b|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}{\rm d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}Q^{2}{\rm d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{4}{\rm d}y\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y+|b|+\|\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla\varepsilon\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Combining the above estimates, we obtain the second estimate in (i).

Proof of (ii). First, differentiating the orthogonality conditions
\left(\varepsilon,Q^{3}\right)=|(\varepsilon,\nabla Q)|=0
in (
3.3
) and then using (
2.14
) and Lemma
3.2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(1+O(b)+O\left(\|\varepsilon e^{-\frac{|y|}{6}})\|_{L^{2}}\right)\right)\left(\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+\left|\frac{x_{1s}}{\lambda}-1\right|+\left|\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}\right|\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim b^{2}+|b_{s}|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\varepsilon|e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}{\rm d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}{\rm d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{3}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}{\rm d}y.

From the 2D Sobolev embedding inequality and (
3.4
), we see that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{3}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}{\rm d}y\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\varepsilon e^{-\frac{|y|}{9}}\|_{L^{2}}\|\varepsilon e^{-\frac{|y|}{9}}\|^{2}_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\varepsilon e^{-\frac{|y|}{9}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|\nabla\left(\varepsilon e^{-\frac{|y|}{9}}\right)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y\right)\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y.

Next, differentiating the orthogonality condition
\left(\varepsilon,Q\right)=0
and then using (
2.15
) and the fact that
\left(\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}\varepsilon,Q\right)=\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)=\left(\Lambda Q,Q\right)=\left|\left(\nabla Q,Q\right)\right|=0
, we obtain

|b_{s}|\lesssim|b|\left(\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right|+\left|\frac{x_{1s}}{\lambda}-1\right|+\left|\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}\right|+|b|\right)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}{\rm d}y+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{3}e^{-\frac{|y|}{3}}{\rm d}y.

Combining the above estimates, we complete the proof of (ii).

Proof of (iii). Note that, for any
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we have

\left|\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right|\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}e^{-\frac{\sqrt{\rho^{2}+y^{2}_{2}}}{4}}{\rm d}\rho\lesssim\left(e^{-\frac{|y_{1}|}{10}}{\textbf{1}}_{(-\infty,0)}(y_{1})+{\textbf{1}}_{[0,\infty)}(y_{1})\right)e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{10}}.

On the other hand, from the definition of
\varphi_{0,B}
, we have

e^{\frac{y_{1}}{B}}\textbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(y_{1})+\left(1+\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)^{6}\right)\textbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y_{1})\lesssim\varphi_{0,B}(y_{1}),\quad\mbox{on}\ \mathbb{R}.

It follows that

\frac{\left|\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right|^{2}}{\varphi_{0,B}(y_{1})}\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}\textbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(y_{1})+\frac{e^{-\frac{|y_{2}|}{10}}}{\left(1+\left(\frac{y_{1}}{B^{10}}\right)^{6}\right)}\textbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(y_{1}).

Combining the above estimates with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\left|\varepsilon(y_{1},y_{2})\right|\left|\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right|\right){\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}y_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\varepsilon\sqrt{\varphi_{0,B}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\left|\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right|^{2}}{\varphi_{0,B}(y_{1})}{\rm d}y_{1}{\rm d}y_{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left(B^{10}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

∎

Note that, for any
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, from Lemma
3.2
and integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\left(\varepsilon(s),\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)

\displaystyle=-\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}f\right)+\left(\Psi_{b},\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)+(R_{b},f)

\displaystyle+\left({\rm{Mod}}-b\Lambda\varepsilon,\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)+\left(R_{NL},f\right).

Therefore, from (
2.9
), (
2.10
), (
3.4
), Lemma
2.3
, Lemma
3.2
, Lemma
3.6
and the 2D Sobolev embedding inequality, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\left(\varepsilon(s),\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)

(3.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}f\right)+\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)\left(\Lambda Q,\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)-b_{s}\left(\frac{\partial Q_{b}}{\partial b},\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}f(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{x_{1s}}{\lambda}-1\right)\left(Q,f\right)+O\left(B^{5}b^{2}+B^{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right).

Last, we deduce the refined modulation estimates for the geometric parameters from the above identity and Lemma
3.6
.

Lemma 3.7
.

In the context of Lemma
3.6
, the following estimates hold.

(i)

Law of
\lambda
.
Let

\sigma_{1}(y)=\frac{1}{\|F\|^{2}_{L^{2}}}\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2})\,{\rm d}\rho\quad\mbox{and}\quad J_{1}(s)=(\varepsilon(s),\sigma_{1}).

Then we have

\left|\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b-2J_{1s}\right|\lesssim B^{5}b^{2}+B^{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y.

(ii)

Law of
b
. Let

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(P,Q)}\left(P(y)+F(y_{2})+h_{2}(y_{2})\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\Lambda P,Q)}{(P,Q)(Q^{3},\Lambda Q)}Q^{3}(y)-c_{1}\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho,

where
F
and
h_{2}
are defined in (
2.1
) and (
2.5
) respectively. In addition, the constant
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
is chosen to ensure that

\left(\sigma_{2},\Lambda Q\right)=\frac{1}{(P,Q)}\left(F+h_{2},\Lambda Q\right)-c_{1}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho,\Lambda Q\right)=0.

We set

g_{2}(y)=\partial_{y_{1}}\sigma_{2}(y)\quad\mbox{and}\quad J_{2}(s)=(\varepsilon(s),\sigma_{2}).

Then we have

|b_{s}+\theta b^{2}+bJ_{2s}|\lesssim B^{5}|b|^{3}+\left(B^{5}|b|+1\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y.

Here, the value of
\theta
is given by (
1.3
).

(iii)

Law of
\frac{b}{\lambda^{\theta}}
. Let

\sigma=2\theta\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad J(s)=(\varepsilon(s),\sigma).

Then we have

\left|\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}s}\left(\frac{b}{\lambda^{\theta}}\right)+\frac{b}{\lambda^{\theta}}J_{s}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda^{\theta}}\left(B^{5}|b|^{3}+\left(B^{5}|b|+1\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right).

(iv)

Law of
x_{2}
. Let

\sigma_{3}(y)=\frac{1}{c_{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\quad\mbox{and}\quad J_{3}(s)=(\varepsilon(s),\sigma_{3}),

where

c_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{y_{2}}Q(y_{1},y_{2}){\rm d}y_{1}\right)^{2}{\rm d}y_{2}.

Then we have

\left|\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}-J_{3s}\right|\lesssim B^{5}b^{2}+B^{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y.

Proof.

Proof of (i). Let
f=\Lambda Q
in (
3.8
). Note that

\mathcal{L}\Lambda Q=-2Q\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left(\Lambda Q,Q\right)=\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)=0.

Therefore, from (
3.8
) and Lemma
3.6
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}J_{1s}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)\left(\Lambda Q,\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\Lambda Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)

\displaystyle+O\left(B^{5}b^{2}+B^{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|F\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)+O\left(B^{5}b^{2}+B^{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right),

which completes the proof of (i).

Proof of (ii). We claim that

\displaystyle b_{s}+\theta b^{2}-\frac{b}{(P,Q)}\frac{(\Lambda P,Q)}{(Q^{3},\Lambda Q)}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}Q^{3})

(3.9)

\displaystyle-\frac{b}{(P,Q)}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}P)=O\left(|b|^{3}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y\right).

Indeed, from Lemma
2.3
, Lemma
3.2
and
\left(\varepsilon,Q^{3}\right)=0
, we have

\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)=\frac{\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}Q^{3}\right)}{\left(\Lambda Q,Q^{3}\right)}+O\left(b^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y\right).

Then, using again Proposition
2.1
, Lemma
2.2
, Lemma
2.3
, Lemma
3.2
and
\left(\varepsilon,Q\right)=|\left(\varepsilon,\nabla Q\right)|=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle b_{s}+\theta b^{2}-\frac{b}{\left(P,Q\right)}\left(\varepsilon,\Lambda Q+6PQ\partial_{y_{1}}Q\right)

\displaystyle-b\left(\frac{\lambda_{s}}{\lambda}+b\right)\frac{\left(\Lambda P,Q\right)}{\left(P,Q\right)}=O\left(|b|^{3}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-\frac{|y|}{10}}{\rm d}y\right).

Note that, from Lemma
2.2
and an elementary computation,

\partial_{y_{1}}\mathcal{L}P=\Lambda Q\Longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}P=\Lambda Q+6PQ\partial_{y_{1}}Q.

We see that (
3.9
) follows from the above three identities.

On the other hand, from the definition of
g_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}
and
P
and
Q
are even in
y_{2}
,

\left(\Lambda Q,\sigma_{2}\right)=\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,\sigma_{2}\right)=(Q,g_{2})=0.

Moreover, using again the definition of
g_{2}
and
\sigma_{2}
, we see that

\lim_{y_{1}\to\infty}\sigma_{2}(y_{1},y_{2})=0,\quad\mbox{for any}\ y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\Longrightarrow\sigma_{2}\left(y_{1},y_{2}\right)=\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}g_{2}(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho.

Therefore, from (
3.8
),
\mathcal{L}\Lambda Q=-2Q
and
\left(\varepsilon,Q\right)=0
, we see that

\displaystyle bJ_{2s}=

\displaystyle-\frac{b}{(P,Q)}\frac{(\Lambda P,Q)}{(Q^{3},\Lambda Q)}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}Q^{3})-\frac{b}{(P,Q)}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{1}}P)

\displaystyle+O\left(B^{5}|b|^{3}+B^{5}|b|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right).

Combining (
3.9
) with the above inequality, we complete the proof of (ii).

Proof of (iii). The estimate in (iii) is a direct consequence of (i) and (ii).

Proof of (iv). Let
f=\partial_{y_{2}}Q
in (
3.8
). Note that

\mathcal{L}\partial_{y_{2}}Q=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left(Q,\partial_{y_{2}}Q\right)=\left(\Lambda Q,\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)=0.

Therefore, using again (
3.8
) and Lemma
3.6
, we obtain

\displaystyle c_{2}J_{3s}

\displaystyle=\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}\left(\partial_{y_{2}}Q,\int_{-\infty}^{y_{1}}\partial_{y_{2}}Q(\rho,y_{2}){\rm d}\rho\right)

\displaystyle+O\left(B^{5}b^{2}+B^{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right)

\displaystyle=c_{2}\frac{x_{2s}}{\lambda}+O\left(B^{5}b^{2}+B^{5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varepsilon^{2}\varphi_{0,B}{\rm d}y\right),

which completes the proof of (iv).
∎

4.
Monotonicity formula

4.1.
Energy estimate

In this subsection, we introduce the weighted energy estimate for the function
\varepsilon
. For
(i,j)\in\left\{1,2\right\}^{2}
, we denote

\mathcal{J}_{i,j}=(1-J_{1})^{-2\theta(j-1)-2i-12}-1,

(4.1)

where
J_{1}
is defined in (i) of Lemma
3.7
